Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main reason I came in is that my head just doesn’t seem to stay on task anymore. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m just being careless, but I’ll be at work doing inventory, counting supplies or checking what needs to be restocked, and then I lose the thread of what I was doing. Like I’ll walk to the supply closet and just stand there for a second because I can’t remember what I came in for.\n\nAnd I’ve been trying to study for a supervisor certification, because I figured that might help me move up a little, but I’ll read the same line over and over and it just doesn’t stick. I know the words are right there, I’m looking at them, but my mind keeps drifting or going blank, and then I get frustrated because it feels like something that should be simple. So… that’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone, because it’s been getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or time with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly, but most days there’s this… heavy, flat kind of feeling that’s just with me. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m not breaking down or anything, but it follows me around.\n\nLike even at work, when I’m just pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, there’s this dull weight in me. And then I’ll go home and sit with my family and try to act normal, try to be present, but inside it still feels kind of gray and distant. It’s like I’m there, but not fully there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, don’t interest you as much lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t know if I’ve noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I stopped liking this thing.” It’s more… I guess I haven’t been paying close attention to what I enjoy or don’t enjoy.\n\nWith friends, I don’t have some big social life where there’s an obvious change to point to. And with hobbies, I mean, I’ll still do things sometimes, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them or just kind of doing them because that’s what I usually do. It’s hard for me to separate being tired or distracted from actually not caring anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I have a good answer. Maybe a little less interested, maybe not. I just know things don’t feel as easy or natural as they used to, but I can’t pin it down to one specific hobby or friendship.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in something, even a small thing? If so, how did it feel compared to how you usually feel?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and… nothing really jumps out as a clear example, which probably sounds bad, but I don’t want to make something up just to have an answer.\n\nThere are little moments where something is okay, I guess. Like if I sit down and drink coffee when the house is quiet, or if something on TV is mildly funny, I can notice it. But I don’t know if I’d call it genuine pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief break in the heaviness, maybe. Not a big feeling of enjoyment where I’m relaxed and absorbed in it.\n\nCompared to how I usually feel, I’d say it’s lighter for a minute, but not really enough that I think, “Oh, I feel good now.” It fades pretty quickly, and then I’m back to just feeling kind of flat. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to look forward to, even small ones, feel completely uninteresting or unenjoyable lately?\n\nPatient: Completely uninteresting… I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels a little too strong for what I can honestly tell you.\n\nIt’s more like things feel muted. Like if there’s something I used to look forward to, I can still sort of recognize, “Oh, this is something I normally like,” but the feeling doesn’t really show up the way I expect it to. Or it shows up very faintly. But I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “this does nothing for me” every single time.\n\nEven with small things, it’s hard to judge. Sometimes I’ll think maybe I should look forward to sitting down after work or watching something or having a quiet minute, and then when I get there, it’s just… fine. Not awful. Not exactly enjoyable either. Just kind of there.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve noticed things don’t feel as satisfying or easy to enjoy, but I’m not sure I’d call them completely unenjoyable. It’s more like the volume is turned way down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often, in the past two weeks, you’ve felt that you couldn’t really enjoy or get interested in things, even ones you used to like? Has it been nearly every day, more than half the days, or less than that?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean count, honestly. Like, if you’re asking me to pick from nearly every day, more than half, or less than that… I keep wanting to say it’s around a lot of days, but then I second-guess whether I’m remembering it right or just describing my general mood.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and make a note of, “Today I couldn’t enjoy things.” It’s more that most days, when I get to something that should be relaxing or interesting, I notice it doesn’t really pull me in much. But then there are little moments where something is okay, so I don’t know if that means it doesn’t count.\n\nI guess if I had to answer, I’d say more than half the days, maybe close to most days, but I’m hesitant to say nearly every day because I’m not totally sure. It’s more often there than not, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, in the past two weeks, you've experienced days where nothing—absolutely nothing—gave you pleasure or enjoyment, not even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely nothing” gave me any pleasure. That feels too definite, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere were definitely days where it felt like nothing really landed, if that makes sense. Like I might put on something I usually like, or make coffee, or just try to sit quietly, and it didn’t feel satisfying. But even on those days, there might have been some tiny moment where something was… not pleasant exactly, but less bad. Like a joke that got a small reaction out of me, or just feeling relieved to sit down for a minute.\n\nSo if you mean full-on enjoyment, like actually feeling good and interested and pleased, then yeah, there have been days where I don’t remember having that. But if you mean literally not one second of anything feeling even slightly okay, I don’t know. I can’t confidently say that. It’s more like everything feels dulled down, not completely absent every single time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you’re not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that clearly. I mean, I do feel tired sometimes, but I’m not sure whether it’s more than usual or just… normal tired, you know? My work is physical enough that it’s hard to separate out. Even if I’m not doing anything intense in the moment, there’s still this background sense of being worn down sometimes, but I can’t tell if that’s an energy thing or just how I usually am.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say, “Yes, I have noticeably less energy,” because I’m not sure I’ve been tracking it that way. Some days I’ll sit down and feel like I don’t really want to get back up, but I don’t know if that’s tiredness exactly. It’s more like I notice it after the fact—like I’ll think, “Why does everything feel like it takes effort?” But then other times I can get through what I need to do.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m not confident saying it’s a definite change. I guess I’ve noticed feeling kind of worn out, but I don’t know how much of that is unusual for me.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to pin that down, because the “even for a short time” part kind of throws me. If you mean like a whole block of the day where I tried to do something I normally like and just couldn’t get into it, then… maybe, yeah, that happens. But if you mean whether I can say there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all, even briefly, I don’t know that I can say that cleanly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll start something and realize I’m just going through the motions. Like I’m doing the thing because it’s what I usually do, not because I’m actually drawn into it. But then sometimes there might be a little flicker of interest or something feels mildly okay for a minute, and then it’s gone. So I have trouble knowing whether that counts as “no interest or pleasure” or just “not much.”\n\nI guess what I can say is there have been days in the past couple weeks where things I usually enjoy didn’t really do much for me. But I’m not totally sure how many, or whether I’d call it none at all.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most activities most of the time” with confidence. That wording feels… bigger than what I can really pin down.\n\nThere have definitely been times where things just feel flat, like I’m doing them because they’re available or because I know they’re things I usually do, not because I’m really pulled toward them. But I’m hesitant to say it’s most things, most of the time, because I haven’t been keeping track like that. Some things feel pointless or kind of empty, and then other times I might get a small bit of distraction or something feels slightly better for a minute.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve noticed less enjoyment, or less of a reaction to things, but I can’t really say whether it rises to “most activities most of the time.” That sounds too exact for what I can honestly report.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you make yourself do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “almost all activities, most of the time” in a really definite way. That sounds like a very strong version of it, and I don’t know that I’ve been experiencing it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are definitely times when I make myself do something and it doesn’t really feel rewarding, or I’m just sort of… doing it because it’s there, or because I usually would, or because I’m trying not to just sit around. But I can’t honestly say that almost everything feels that way most of the time. Some things still might hold my attention a little, or I might get a small amount of satisfaction from getting through something, even if it’s not much.\n\nSo I’d say there’s been some loss of interest or pleasure, yeah, but I’m not sure it’s at the level you’re describing. “Almost all activities, most of the time” feels too certain for me to agree to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything takes more effort or feels heavy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m moving slower or anything like that.\n\nSometimes things feel like they take effort, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically slowed down. With work, for example, I’m still doing what I need to do, but I might feel like I’m pushing through it more than I used to. But I can’t say my thoughts are noticeably slow, like my brain is moving in slow motion or I can’t get words out.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some heaviness in the sense that getting started on things can feel like more of a chore, but I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower than usual. I’d probably have to think about that more or have someone else tell me if they’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. And on days when I’m not doing much physically, I don’t know… I might still feel kind of low-energy or like I’m dragging a bit, but I can’t say for sure that it’s almost every day or that it stands out as a big change.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe some tiredness, but I’m not confident calling it a noticeable fatigue almost every day. I’d have to pay closer attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of the time, you’ve felt like nothing really interested you or gave you pleasure, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still having a hard time saying yes to “most of the time” and “nothing really interested me.” That feels a little too absolute for what I’ve noticed.\n\nI do have stretches where things feel kind of flat, like I’ll do something I normally might enjoy and it doesn’t really land the same way. Like a joke that got a small reaction out of me, or just feeling relieved to sit down for a minute.\n\nSo if you mean full-on enjoyment, like actually feeling good and interested and pleased, then yeah, there have been days where I don’t remember having that. But if you mean literally not one second of anything feeling even slightly okay, I don’t know. I can’t confidently say that. It’s more like everything feels dulled down, not completely absent every single time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you’re not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that clearly. I mean, I do feel tired sometimes, but I’m not sure whether it’s more than usual or just… normal tired, you know? My work is physical enough that it’s hard to separate out. Even if I’m not doing anything intense in the moment, there’s still this background sense of being worn down sometimes, but I can’t tell if that’s an energy thing or just how I usually am.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say, “Yes, I have noticeably less energy,” because I’m not sure I’ve been tracking it that way. Some days I’ll sit down and feel like I don’t really want to get back up, but I don’t know if that’s tiredness exactly. It’s more like I notice it after the fact—like I’ll think, “Why does everything feel like it takes effort?” But then other times I can get through what I need to do.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m not confident saying it’s a definite change. I guess I’ve noticed feeling kind of worn out, but I don’t know how much of that is unusual for me.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to pin that down, because the “even for a short time” part kind of throws me. If you mean like a whole block of the day where I tried to do something I normally like and just couldn’t get into it, then… maybe, yeah, that happens. But if you mean whether I can say there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all, even briefly, I don’t know that I can say that cleanly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll start something and realize I’m just going through the motions. Like I’m doing the thing because it’s what I usually do, not because I’m actually drawn into it. But then sometimes there might be a little flicker of interest or something feels mildly okay for a minute, and then it’s gone. So I have trouble knowing whether that counts as “no interest or pleasure” or just “not much.”\n\nI guess what I can say is there have been days in the past couple weeks where things I usually enjoy didn’t really do much for me. But I’m not totally sure how many, or whether I’d call it none at all.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most activities most of the time” with confidence. That wording feels… bigger than what I can really pin down.\n\nThere have definitely been times where things just feel flat, like I’m doing them because they’re available or because I know they’re things I usually do, not because I’m really pulled toward them. But I’m hesitant to say it’s most things, most of the time, because I haven’t been keeping track like that. Some things feel pointless or kind of empty, and then other times I might get a small bit of distraction or something feels slightly better for a minute.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve noticed less enjoyment, or less of a reaction to things, but I can’t really say whether it rises to “most activities most of the time.” That sounds too exact for what I can honestly report.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you make yourself do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “almost all activities, most of the time” in a really definite way. That sounds like a very strong version of it, and I don’t know that I’ve been experiencing it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are definitely times when I make myself do something and it doesn’t really feel rewarding, or I’m just sort of… doing it because it’s there, or because I usually would, or because I’m trying not to just sit around. But I can’t honestly say that almost everything feels that way most of the time. Some things still might hold my attention a little, or I might get a small amount of satisfaction from getting through something, even if it’s not much.\n\nSo I’d say there’s been some loss of interest or pleasure, yeah, but I’m not sure it’s at the level you’re describing. “Almost all activities, most of the time” feels too certain for me to agree to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything takes more effort or feels heavy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m moving slower or anything like that.\n\nSometimes things feel like they take effort, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically slowed down. With work, for example, I’m still doing what I need to do, but I might feel like I’m pushing through it more than I used to. But I can’t say my thoughts are noticeably slow, like my brain is moving in slow motion or I can’t get words out.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some heaviness in the sense that getting started on things can feel like more of a chore, but I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower than usual. I’d probably have to think about that more or have someone else tell me if they’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. It’s more often there than not, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, in the past two weeks, you've experienced days where nothing—absolutely nothing—gave you pleasure or enjoyment, not even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely nothing” gave me any pleasure. That feels too definite, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere were definitely days where it felt like nothing really landed, if that makes sense. Like I might put on something I usually like, or make coffee, or just try to sit quietly, and it didn’t feel satisfying. But even on those days, there might have been some tiny moment where something was… not pleasant exactly, but less bad. Like a joke that got a small reaction out of me, or just feeling relieved to sit down for a minute.\n\nSo if you mean full-on enjoyment, like actually feeling good and interested and pleased, then yeah, there have been days where I don’t remember having that. But if you mean literally not one second of anything feeling even slightly okay, I don’t know. I can’t confidently say that. It’s more like everything feels dulled down, not completely absent every single time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you’re not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that clearly. I mean, I do feel tired sometimes, but I’m not sure whether it’s more than usual or just… normal tired, you know? My work is physical enough that it’s hard to separate out. Even if I’m not doing anything intense in the moment, there’s still this background sense of being worn down sometimes, but I can’t tell if that’s an energy thing or just how I usually am.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say, “Yes, I have noticeably less energy,” because I’m not sure I’ve been tracking it that way. Some days I’ll sit down and feel like I don’t really want to get back up, but I don’t know if that’s tiredness exactly. It’s more like I notice it after the fact—like I’ll think, “Why does everything feel like it takes effort?” But then other times I can get through what I need to do.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m not confident saying it’s a definite change. I guess I’ve noticed feeling kind of worn out, but I don’t know how much of that is unusual for me.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to pin that down, because the “even for a short time” part kind of throws me. If you mean like a whole block of the day where I tried to do something I normally like and just couldn’t get into it, then… maybe, yeah, that happens. But if you mean whether I can say there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all, even briefly, I don’t know that I can say that cleanly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll start something and realize I’m just going through the motions. Like I’m doing the thing because it’s what I usually do, not because I’m actually drawn into it. But then sometimes there might be a little flicker of interest or something feels mildly okay for a minute, and then it’s gone. So I have trouble knowing whether that counts as “no interest or pleasure” or just “not much.”\n\nI guess what I can say is there have been days in the past couple weeks where things I usually enjoy didn’t really do much for me. But I’m not totally sure how many, or whether I’d call it none at all.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most activities most of the time” with confidence. That wording feels… bigger than what I can really pin down.\n\nThere have definitely been times where things just feel flat, like I’m doing them because they’re available or because I know they’re things I usually do, not because I’m really pulled toward them. But I’m hesitant to say it’s most things, most of the time, because I haven’t been keeping track like that. Some things feel pointless or kind of empty, and then other times I might get a small bit of distraction or something feels slightly better for a minute.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve noticed less enjoyment, or less of a reaction to things, but I can’t really say whether it rises to “most activities most of the time.” That sounds too exact for what I can honestly report.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you make yourself do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “almost all activities, most of the time” in a really definite way. That sounds like a very strong version of it, and I don’t know that I’ve been experiencing it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are definitely times when I make myself do something and it doesn’t really feel rewarding, or I’m just sort of… doing it because it’s there, or because I usually would, or because I’m trying not to just sit around. But I can’t honestly say that almost everything feels that way most of the time. Some things still might hold my attention a little, or I might get a small amount of satisfaction from getting through something, even if it’s not much.\n\nSo I’d say there’s been some loss of interest or pleasure, yeah, but I’m not sure it’s at the level you’re describing. “Almost all activities, most of the time” feels too certain for me to agree to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything takes more effort or feels heavy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m moving slower or anything like that.\n\nSometimes things feel like they take effort, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically slowed down. With work, for example, I’m still doing what I need to do, but I might feel like I’m pushing through it more than I used to. But I can’t say my thoughts are noticeably slow, like my brain is moving in slow motion or I can’t get words out.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some heaviness in the sense that getting started on things can feel like more of a chore, but I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower than usual. I’d probably have to think about that more or have someone else tell me if they’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. And on days when I’m not doing much physically, I don’t know… I might still feel kind of low-energy or like I’m dragging a bit, but I can’t say for sure that it’s almost every day or that it stands out as a big change.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe some tiredness, but I’m not confident calling it a noticeable fatigue almost every day. I’d have to pay closer attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of the time, you’ve felt like nothing really interested you or gave you pleasure, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still having a hard time saying yes to “most of the time” and “nothing really interested me.” That feels a little too absolute for what I’ve noticed.\n\nI do have stretches where things feel kind of flat, like I’ll do something I normally might enjoy and it doesn’t really land the same way. I’d say it comes and goes, and there’s maybe less enjoyment than I’d like, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as that severe.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost everything you do, most of the time, even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clear loss in almost everything, no. I’m… still kind of stuck on the “most of my activities, most of the time” part. I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not trying to dodge the question. It’s just hard for me to sort it into that clean of a box.

I’d start something and not really get that much out of it, or I’d think, “Why am I even bothering?” But then other times, I could still get through something and feel a little engaged, or at least not totally uninterested. So I don’t know that I can honestly say most of my activities were mostly uninteresting most of the time.

Maybe if you asked whether enjoyment has felt kind of weaker than I’d like, I’d say yes, probably. But the way you’re asking it — like it was the overall pattern across most things, most of the time — I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.

I haven’t noticed it as this clear, across-the-board loss where nothing matters or nothing feels good most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities, most of the time—even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can say it that strongly. I hear what you’re asking — like, not whether I had a couple decent moments, but whether overall most things felt kind of empty or not enjoyable most of the time. And… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying attention in a way where I could give a clean yes or no.\n\nThere are definitely times where I’m doing something and it feels kind of pointless or flat, like I’m just doing it because it’s there. But I don’t know if that adds up to “most activities, most of the time.” That sounds like a level of certainty I don’t really have.\n\nI guess what I can say is that enjoyment hasn’t felt very strong or easy to come by, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’ve lost interest in most things most of the time. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of your activities felt mostly uninteresting or without much pleasure most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I’m… still kind of stuck on the “most of my activities, most of the time” part. I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not trying to dodge the question. It’s just hard for me to sort it into that clean of a box.\n\nLike, if I think about the last couple weeks, there were definitely things that felt dull or like I was just doing them without much feeling behind it. I’d start something and not really get that much out of it, or I’d think, “Why am I even bothering?” But then other times, I could still get through something and feel a little engaged, or at least not totally uninterested. So I don’t know that I can honestly say most of my activities were mostly uninteresting most of the time.\n\nMaybe if you asked whether enjoyment has felt kind of weaker than I’d like, I’d say yes, probably. But the way you’re asking it — like it was the overall pattern across most things, most of the time — I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a marked loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you try to engage in them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can say “marked” or “almost all activities,” no. I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m sorry if I’m not giving the kind of definite answer that helps, but that wording still feels too strong for what I’ve actually noticed.

When I try to do things, sometimes it does feel flat. Like I’ll put something on, or try to get into a task, or do something I’d normally expect to enjoy, and there’s this feeling of… not much happening inside. But I can’t honestly say that happens with almost everything, most of the time. It’s not like every activity feels dead or completely pointless once I try it.

So I guess my answer is: I’m not sure, but probably not in that marked, across-the-board way. Enjoyment can feel dulled, but I haven’t noticed a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities most of the time.

Like if there’s something I used to look forward to, I can still sort of recognize, “Oh, this is something I normally like,” but the feeling doesn’t really show up the way I expect it to. Has it been nearly every day, more than half the days, or less than that?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean count, honestly. Like, if you’re asking me to pick from nearly every day, more than half, or less than that… I keep wanting to say it’s around a lot of days, but then I second-guess whether I’m remembering it right or just describing my general mood.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and make a note of, “Today I couldn’t enjoy things.” It’s more that most days, when I get to something that should be relaxing or interesting, I notice it doesn’t really pull me in much. But then there are little moments where something is okay, so I don’t know if that means it doesn’t count.\n\nI guess if I had to answer, I’d say more than half the days, maybe close to most days, but I’m hesitant to say nearly every day because I’m not totally sure. It’s more often there than not, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, in the past two weeks, you've experienced days where nothing—absolutely nothing—gave you pleasure or enjoyment, not even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely nothing” gave me any pleasure. That feels too definite, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere were definitely days where it felt like nothing really landed, if that makes sense. Like I might put on something I usually like, or make coffee, or just try to sit quietly, and it didn’t feel satisfying. But even on those days, there might have been some tiny moment where something was… not pleasant exactly, but less bad. Like a joke that got a small reaction out of me, or just feeling relieved to sit down for a minute.\n\nSo if you mean full-on enjoyment, like actually feeling good and interested and pleased, then yeah, there have been days where I don’t remember having that. But if you mean literally not one second of anything feeling even slightly okay, I don’t know. I can’t confidently say that. It’s more like everything feels dulled down, not completely absent every single time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you’re not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that clearly. Even if I’m not doing anything intense in the moment, there’s still this background sense of being worn down sometimes, but I can’t tell if that’s an energy thing or just how I usually am.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say, “Yes, I have noticeably less energy,” because I’m not sure I’ve been tracking it that way. Yeah, my sleep has definitely been off. I don’t know if I’d describe it neatly as one thing, because it sort of depends on the day, but after my overnight shifts especially, I’ll get home and I’m tired — like physically tired — but my body doesn’t seem to know how to settle down. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main reason I came in is that my head just doesn’t seem to stay on task anymore. It’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m just being careless, but I’ll be at work doing inventory, counting supplies or checking what needs to be restocked, and then I lose the thread of what I was doing. Like I’ll walk to the supply closet and just stand there for a second because I can’t remember what I came in for.\n\nAnd I’ve been trying to study for a supervisor certification, because I figured that might help me move up a little, but I’ll read the same line over and over and it just doesn’t stick. I know the words are right there, I’m looking at them, but my mind keeps drifting or going blank, and then I get frustrated because it feels like something that should be simple. So… that’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone, because it’s been getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or time with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly, but most days there’s this… heavy, flat kind of feeling that’s just with me. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m not breaking down or anything, but it follows me around.\n\nLike even at work, when I’m just pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, there’s this dull weight in me. And then I’ll go home and sit with my family and try to act normal, try to be present, but inside it still feels kind of gray and distant. It’s like I’m there, but not fully there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, don’t interest you as much lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t know if I’ve noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I stopped liking this thing.” It’s more… I guess I haven’t been paying close attention to what I enjoy or don’t enjoy.\n\nWith friends, I don’t have some big social life where there’s an obvious change to point to. And with hobbies, I mean, I’ll still do things sometimes, but I don’t always know if I’m enjoying them or just kind of doing them because that’s what I usually do. It’s hard for me to separate being tired or distracted from actually not caring anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I have a good answer. Maybe a little less interested, maybe not. I just know things don’t feel as easy or natural as they used to, but I can’t pin it down to one specific hobby or friendship.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in something, even a small thing? If so, how did it feel compared to how you usually feel?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and… nothing really jumps out as a clear example, which probably sounds bad, but I don’t want to make something up just to have an answer.\n\nThere are little moments where something is okay, I guess. Like if I sit down and drink coffee when the house is quiet, or if something on TV is mildly funny, I can notice it. But I don’t know if I’d call it genuine pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief break in the heaviness, maybe. Not a big feeling of enjoyment where I’m relaxed and absorbed in it.\n\nCompared to how I usually feel, I’d say it’s lighter for a minute, but not really enough that I think, “Oh, I feel good now.” It fades pretty quickly, and then I’m back to just feeling kind of flat. I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to look forward to, even small ones, feel completely uninteresting or unenjoyable lately?\n\nPatient: Completely uninteresting… I don’t know if I’d say completely. That word feels a little too strong for what I can honestly tell you.\n\nIt’s more like things feel muted. Like if there’s something I used to look forward to, I can still sort of recognize, “Oh, this is something I normally like,” but the feeling doesn’t really show up the way I expect it to. Or it shows up very faintly. But I’m not sitting there thinking, “I hate this” or “this does nothing for me” every single time.\n\nEven with small things, it’s hard to judge. Sometimes I’ll think maybe I should look forward to sitting down after work or watching something or having a quiet minute, and then when I get there, it’s just… fine. Not awful. Not exactly enjoyable either. Just kind of there.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve noticed things don’t feel as satisfying or easy to enjoy, but I’m not sure I’d call them completely unenjoyable. It’s more like the volume is turned way down, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often, in the past two weeks, you’ve felt that you couldn’t really enjoy or get interested in things, even ones you used to like? Has it been nearly every day, more than half the days, or less than that?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean count, honestly. Like, if you’re asking me to pick from nearly every day, more than half, or less than that… I keep wanting to say it’s around a lot of days, but then I second-guess whether I’m remembering it right or just describing my general mood.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and make a note of, “Today I couldn’t enjoy things.” It’s more that most days, when I get to something that should be relaxing or interesting, I notice it doesn’t really pull me in much. But then there are little moments where something is okay, so I don’t know if that means it doesn’t count.\n\nI guess if I had to answer, I’d say more than half the days, maybe close to most days, but I’m hesitant to say nearly every day because I’m not totally sure. It’s more often there than not, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, in the past two weeks, you've experienced days where nothing—absolutely nothing—gave you pleasure or enjoyment, not even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely nothing” gave me any pleasure. That feels too definite, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere were definitely days where it felt like nothing really landed, if that makes sense. Like I might put on something I usually like, or make coffee, or just try to sit quietly, and it didn’t feel satisfying. But even on those days, there might have been some tiny moment where something was… not pleasant exactly, but less bad. Like a joke that got a small reaction out of me, or just feeling relieved to sit down for a minute.\n\nSo if you mean full-on enjoyment, like actually feeling good and interested and pleased, then yeah, there have been days where I don’t remember having that. But if you mean literally not one second of anything feeling even slightly okay, I don’t know. I can’t confidently say that. It’s more like everything feels dulled down, not completely absent every single time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you’re not doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can answer that clearly. I mean, I do feel tired sometimes, but I’m not sure whether it’s more than usual or just… normal tired, you know? My work is physical enough that it’s hard to separate out. Even if I’m not doing anything intense in the moment, there’s still this background sense of being worn down sometimes, but I can’t tell if that’s an energy thing or just how I usually am.\n\nI wouldn’t want to say, “Yes, I have noticeably less energy,” because I’m not sure I’ve been tracking it that way. Some days I’ll sit down and feel like I don’t really want to get back up, but I don’t know if that’s tiredness exactly. It’s more like I notice it after the fact—like I’ll think, “Why does everything feel like it takes effort?” But then other times I can get through what I need to do.\n\nSo… maybe a little? But I’m not confident saying it’s a definite change. I guess I’ve noticed feeling kind of worn out, but I don’t know how much of that is unusual for me.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have there been days when you felt no interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even for a short time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to pin that down, because the “even for a short time” part kind of throws me. If you mean like a whole block of the day where I tried to do something I normally like and just couldn’t get into it, then… maybe, yeah, that happens. But if you mean whether I can say there was absolutely no interest or pleasure at all, even briefly, I don’t know that I can say that cleanly.\n\nIt’s more that I’ll start something and realize I’m just going through the motions. Like I’m doing the thing because it’s what I usually do, not because I’m actually drawn into it. But then sometimes there might be a little flicker of interest or something feels mildly okay for a minute, and then it’s gone. So I have trouble knowing whether that counts as “no interest or pleasure” or just “not much.”\n\nI guess what I can say is there have been days in the past couple weeks where things I usually enjoy didn’t really do much for me. But I’m not totally sure how many, or whether I’d call it none at all.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most activities most of the time” with confidence. That wording feels… bigger than what I can really pin down.\n\nThere have definitely been times where things just feel flat, like I’m doing them because they’re available or because I know they’re things I usually do, not because I’m really pulled toward them. But I’m hesitant to say it’s most things, most of the time, because I haven’t been keeping track like that. Some things feel pointless or kind of empty, and then other times I might get a small bit of distraction or something feels slightly better for a minute.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve noticed less enjoyment, or less of a reaction to things, but I can’t really say whether it rises to “most activities most of the time.” That sounds too exact for what I can honestly report.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you make yourself do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “almost all activities, most of the time” in a really definite way. That sounds like a very strong version of it, and I don’t know that I’ve been experiencing it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are definitely times when I make myself do something and it doesn’t really feel rewarding, or I’m just sort of… doing it because it’s there, or because I usually would, or because I’m trying not to just sit around. But I can’t honestly say that almost everything feels that way most of the time. Some things still might hold my attention a little, or I might get a small amount of satisfaction from getting through something, even if it’s not much.\n\nSo I’d say there’s been some loss of interest or pleasure, yeah, but I’m not sure it’s at the level you’re describing. “Almost all activities, most of the time” feels too certain for me to agree to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything takes more effort or feels heavy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m moving slower or anything like that.\n\nSometimes things feel like they take effort, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically slowed down. With work, for example, I’m still doing what I need to do, but I might feel like I’m pushing through it more than I used to. But I can’t say my thoughts are noticeably slow, like my brain is moving in slow motion or I can’t get words out.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some heaviness in the sense that getting started on things can feel like more of a chore, but I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower than usual. I’d probably have to think about that more or have someone else tell me if they’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel tired sometimes, but “noticeable loss of energy almost every day” feels like a pretty specific thing, and I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it that closely.\n\nWith my job, I’m on my feet and doing physical stuff, so it’s hard for me to separate normal tired from something more than that. There are days where I get home and I’m worn out, but that could just be from working. And on days when I’m not doing much physically, I don’t know… I might still feel kind of low-energy or like I’m dragging a bit, but I can’t say for sure that it’s almost every day or that it stands out as a big change.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe some tiredness, but I’m not confident calling it a noticeable fatigue almost every day. I’d have to pay closer attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of the time, you’ve felt like nothing really interested you or gave you pleasure, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still having a hard time saying yes to “most of the time” and “nothing really interested me.” That feels a little too absolute for what I’ve noticed.\n\nI do have stretches where things feel kind of flat, like I’ll do something I normally might enjoy and it doesn’t really land the same way. But it’s not like every single thing is completely dead to me, if that makes sense. Sometimes I can still get into something a little bit, or at least feel okay while I’m doing it, even if it’s not this big enjoyable feeling.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say most of the time nothing interests me or gives me pleasure. I’d say it comes and goes, and there’s maybe less enjoyment than I’d like, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as that severe.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost everything you do, most of the time, even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clear loss in almost everything, no. I mean, like I was saying, there are definitely times where things feel kind of muted or like I’m just going through the motions, but “almost everything, most of the time” sounds stronger than what I can honestly say.\n\nIt’s more like… if I do something I usually like, sometimes I’m not as into it as I think I should be, or I notice I’m not getting much out of it. But other times I can still be somewhat interested, or at least distracted in a decent way. So I don’t want to say I’ve lost interest in everything, because that doesn’t feel accurate.\n\nI guess the best way I can put it is that enjoyment feels less reliable, maybe, but not completely gone. I haven’t noticed it as this clear, across-the-board loss where nothing matters or nothing feels good most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities, most of the time—even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can say it that strongly. I hear what you’re asking — like, not whether I had a couple decent moments, but whether overall most things felt kind of empty or not enjoyable most of the time. And… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying attention in a way where I could give a clean yes or no.\n\nThere are definitely times where I’m doing something and it feels kind of pointless or flat, like I’m just doing it because it’s there. But I don’t know if that adds up to “most activities, most of the time.” That sounds like a level of certainty I don’t really have.\n\nI guess what I can say is that enjoyment hasn’t felt very strong or easy to come by, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’ve lost interest in most things most of the time. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of your activities felt mostly uninteresting or without much pleasure most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I’m… still kind of stuck on the “most of my activities, most of the time” part. I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not trying to dodge the question. It’s just hard for me to sort it into that clean of a box.\n\nLike, if I think about the last couple weeks, there were definitely things that felt dull or like I was just doing them without much feeling behind it. I’d start something and not really get that much out of it, or I’d think, “Why am I even bothering?” But then other times, I could still get through something and feel a little engaged, or at least not totally uninterested. So I don’t know that I can honestly say most of my activities were mostly uninteresting most of the time.\n\nMaybe if you asked whether enjoyment has felt kind of weaker than I’d like, I’d say yes, probably. But the way you’re asking it — like it was the overall pattern across most things, most of the time — I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a marked loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you try to engage in them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “marked” or “almost all activities,” no. I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m sorry if I’m not giving the kind of definite answer that helps, but that wording still feels too strong for what I’ve actually noticed.\n\nWhen I try to do things, sometimes it does feel flat. Like I’ll put something on, or try to get into a task, or do something I’d normally expect to enjoy, and there’s this feeling of… not much happening inside. But I can’t honestly say that happens with almost everything, most of the time. It’s not like every activity feels dead or completely pointless once I try it.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure, but probably not in that marked, across-the-board way. Enjoyment can feel dulled, but I haven’t noticed a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve had trouble sleeping — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up much earlier than you should — nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my sleep has definitely been off. I don’t know if I’d describe it neatly as one thing, because it sort of depends on the day, but after my overnight shifts especially, I’ll get home and I’m tired — like physically tired — but my body doesn’t seem to know how to settle down. I’ll lie there feeling wired, like I should be asleep but I can’t really switch off.\n\nAnd even when I do sleep what looks like a decent number of hours, it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I’ll wake up and feel like I barely rested, like I was just unconscious for a while but not actually restored. So yeah, trouble sleeping has been a pretty regular thing for me. It’s not new just in the last two weeks, but it has been going on during the last two weeks too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But then sometimes there might be a little flicker of interest or something feels mildly okay for a minute, and then it’s gone. So I have trouble knowing whether that counts as “no interest or pleasure” or just “not much.”\n\nI guess what I can say is there have been days in the past couple weeks where things I usually enjoy didn’t really do much for me. But I’m not totally sure how many, or whether I’d call it none at all.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most activities most of the time” with confidence. That wording feels… bigger than what I can really pin down.\n\nThere have definitely been times where things just feel flat, like I’m doing them because they’re available or because I know they’re things I usually do, not because I’m really pulled toward them. But I’m hesitant to say it’s most things, most of the time, because I haven’t been keeping track like that. Some things feel pointless or kind of empty, and then other times I might get a small bit of distraction or something feels slightly better for a minute.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I’ve noticed less enjoyment, or less of a reaction to things, but I can’t really say whether it rises to “most activities most of the time.” That sounds too exact for what I can honestly report.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you make yourself do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “almost all activities, most of the time” in a really definite way. That sounds like a very strong version of it, and I don’t know that I’ve been experiencing it that clearly.\n\nLike, there are definitely times when I make myself do something and it doesn’t really feel rewarding, or I’m just sort of… doing it because it’s there, or because I usually would, or because I’m trying not to just sit around. But I can’t honestly say that almost everything feels that way most of the time. Some things still might hold my attention a little, or I might get a small amount of satisfaction from getting through something, even if it’s not much.\n\nSo I’d say there’s been some loss of interest or pleasure, yeah, but I’m not sure it’s at the level you’re describing. “Almost all activities, most of the time” feels too certain for me to agree to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything takes more effort or feels heavy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m moving slower or anything like that.\n\nSometimes things feel like they take effort, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically slowed down. With work, for example, I’m still doing what I need to do, but I might feel like I’m pushing through it more than I used to. But I can’t say my thoughts are noticeably slow, like my brain is moving in slow motion or I can’t get words out.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some heaviness in the sense that getting started on things can feel like more of a chore, but I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower than usual. I’d probably have to think about that more or have someone else tell me if they’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel tired sometimes, but “noticeable loss of energy almost every day” feels like a pretty specific thing, and I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it that closely.\n\nWith my job, I’m on my feet and doing physical stuff, so it’s hard for me to separate normal tired from something more than that. There are days where I get home and I’m worn out, but that could just be from working. And on days when I’m not doing much physically, I don’t know… I might still feel kind of low-energy or like I’m dragging a bit, but I can’t say for sure that it’s almost every day or that it stands out as a big change.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe some tiredness, but I’m not confident calling it a noticeable fatigue almost every day. I’d have to pay closer attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of the time, you’ve felt like nothing really interested you or gave you pleasure, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still having a hard time saying yes to “most of the time” and “nothing really interested me.” That feels a little too absolute for what I’ve noticed.\n\nI do have stretches where things feel kind of flat, like I’ll do something I normally might enjoy and it doesn’t really land the same way. But it’s not like every single thing is completely dead to me, if that makes sense. Sometimes I can still get into something a little bit, or at least feel okay while I’m doing it, even if it’s not this big enjoyable feeling.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say most of the time nothing interests me or gives me pleasure. I’d say it comes and goes, and there’s maybe less enjoyment than I’d like, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as that severe.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost everything you do, most of the time, even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clear loss in almost everything, no. I mean, like I was saying, there are definitely times where things feel kind of muted or like I’m just going through the motions, but “almost everything, most of the time” sounds stronger than what I can honestly say.\n\nIt’s more like… if I do something I usually like, sometimes I’m not as into it as I think I should be, or I notice I’m not getting much out of it. But other times I can still be somewhat interested, or at least distracted in a decent way. So I don’t want to say I’ve lost interest in everything, because that doesn’t feel accurate.\n\nI guess the best way I can put it is that enjoyment feels less reliable, maybe, but not completely gone. I haven’t noticed it as this clear, across-the-board loss where nothing matters or nothing feels good most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities, most of the time—even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can say it that strongly. I’m not really sure on that one. I haven’t noticed a clear decrease in my appetite, like nothing where I’m thinking, “Oh, I’m barely eating now,” or anything that obvious.

It’s more often there than not, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, in the past two weeks, you've experienced days where nothing—absolutely nothing—gave you pleasure or enjoyment, not even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “absolutely nothing” gave me any pleasure. Some things still might hold my attention a little, or I might get a small amount of satisfaction from getting through something, even if it’s not much.\n\nSo I’d say there’s been some loss of interest or pleasure, yeah, but I’m not sure it’s at the level you’re describing. “Almost all activities, most of the time” feels too certain for me to agree to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down, or that you're moving more slowly than usual, like everything takes more effort or feels heavy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, like I don’t know if someone watching me would say I’m moving slower or anything like that.\n\nSometimes things feel like they take effort, but I don’t know if that’s the same as being physically slowed down. With work, for example, I’m still doing what I need to do, but I might feel like I’m pushing through it more than I used to. But I can’t say my thoughts are noticeably slow, like my brain is moving in slow motion or I can’t get words out.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some heaviness in the sense that getting started on things can feel like more of a chore, but I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower than usual. I’d probably have to think about that more or have someone else tell me if they’ve seen it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve felt a noticeable loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been doing anything physically demanding?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel tired sometimes, but “noticeable loss of energy almost every day” feels like a pretty specific thing, and I don’t know that I’ve been tracking it that closely.\n\nWith my job, I’m on my feet and doing physical stuff, so it’s hard for me to separate normal tired from something more than that. There are days where I get home and I’m worn out, but that could just be from working. And on days when I’m not doing much physically, I don’t know… I might still feel kind of low-energy or like I’m dragging a bit, but I can’t say for sure that it’s almost every day or that it stands out as a big change.\n\nSo I guess I’d say maybe some tiredness, but I’m not confident calling it a noticeable fatigue almost every day. I’d have to pay closer attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of the time, you’ve felt like nothing really interested you or gave you pleasure, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still having a hard time saying yes to “most of the time” and “nothing really interested me.” That feels a little too absolute for what I’ve noticed.\n\nI do have stretches where things feel kind of flat, like I’ll do something I normally might enjoy and it doesn’t really land the same way. But it’s not like every single thing is completely dead to me, if that makes sense. Sometimes I can still get into something a little bit, or at least feel okay while I’m doing it, even if it’s not this big enjoyable feeling.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say most of the time nothing interests me or gives me pleasure. I’d say it comes and goes, and there’s maybe less enjoyment than I’d like, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as that severe.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost everything you do, most of the time, even things you used to like?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a clear loss in almost everything, no. I mean, like I was saying, there are definitely times where things feel kind of muted or like I’m just going through the motions, but “almost everything, most of the time” sounds stronger than what I can honestly say.\n\nIt’s more like… if I do something I usually like, sometimes I’m not as into it as I think I should be, or I notice I’m not getting much out of it. But other times I can still be somewhat interested, or at least distracted in a decent way. So I don’t want to say I’ve lost interest in everything, because that doesn’t feel accurate.\n\nI guess the best way I can put it is that enjoyment feels less reliable, maybe, but not completely gone. I haven’t noticed it as this clear, across-the-board loss where nothing matters or nothing feels good most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in most activities, most of the time—even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can say it that strongly. I hear what you’re asking — like, not whether I had a couple decent moments, but whether overall most things felt kind of empty or not enjoyable most of the time. And… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve been paying attention in a way where I could give a clean yes or no.\n\nThere are definitely times where I’m doing something and it feels kind of pointless or flat, like I’m just doing it because it’s there. But I don’t know if that adds up to “most activities, most of the time.” That sounds like a level of certainty I don’t really have.\n\nI guess what I can say is that enjoyment hasn’t felt very strong or easy to come by, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say I’ve lost interest in most things most of the time. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that most of your activities felt mostly uninteresting or without much pleasure most of the time, even if there were brief moments when things felt slightly better?\n\nPatient: I’m… still kind of stuck on the “most of my activities, most of the time” part. I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not trying to dodge the question. It’s just hard for me to sort it into that clean of a box.\n\nLike, if I think about the last couple weeks, there were definitely things that felt dull or like I was just doing them without much feeling behind it. I’d start something and not really get that much out of it, or I’d think, “Why am I even bothering?” But then other times, I could still get through something and feel a little engaged, or at least not totally uninterested. So I don’t know that I can honestly say most of my activities were mostly uninteresting most of the time.\n\nMaybe if you asked whether enjoyment has felt kind of weaker than I’d like, I’d say yes, probably. But the way you’re asking it — like it was the overall pattern across most things, most of the time — I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve experienced a marked loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities, most of the time, even when you try to engage in them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “marked” or “almost all activities,” no. I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m sorry if I’m not giving the kind of definite answer that helps, but that wording still feels too strong for what I’ve actually noticed.\n\nWhen I try to do things, sometimes it does feel flat. Like I’ll put something on, or try to get into a task, or do something I’d normally expect to enjoy, and there’s this feeling of… not much happening inside. But I can’t honestly say that happens with almost everything, most of the time. It’s not like every activity feels dead or completely pointless once I try it.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure, but probably not in that marked, across-the-board way. Enjoyment can feel dulled, but I haven’t noticed a clear loss of interest or pleasure in almost all activities most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that you’ve had trouble sleeping — either not being able to fall asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up much earlier than you should — nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my sleep has definitely been off. I don’t know if I’d describe it neatly as one thing, because it sort of depends on the day, but after my overnight shifts especially, I’ll get home and I’m tired — like physically tired — but my body doesn’t seem to know how to settle down. I’ll lie there feeling wired, like I should be asleep but I can’t really switch off.\n\nAnd even when I do sleep what looks like a decent number of hours, it doesn’t feel like good sleep. I’ll wake up and feel like I barely rested, like I was just unconscious for a while but not actually restored. So yeah, trouble sleeping has been a pretty regular thing for me. It’s not new just in the last two weeks, but it has been going on during the last two weeks too.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, would you say that your appetite has decreased noticeably, or that you've lost weight without trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I haven’t noticed a clear decrease in my appetite, like nothing where I’m thinking, “Oh, I’m barely eating now,” or anything that obvious.\n\nMy eating can be kind of irregular just because of my schedule and the way my days are structured, so sometimes I’ll skip around meals or eat at odd times, but I don’t know that it’s because I’ve lost my appetite. And I haven’t noticed my clothes fitting differently or a number on the scale changing — I don’t weigh myself that regularly, though, so I can’t say with total certainty.\n\nSo… I guess my answer would be no, not noticeably. At least not anything I’ve picked up on."
